_source line 1756 · 2026-05-19T01:47:12.140Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01W2hMuPTMaSqSRevktqYvT5`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
4	A Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation with 30 task types, faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}
5	
6	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.
7	Toolformer \cite{toolformer-2023} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \cite{chameleon-2023} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.
8	More recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \cite{mtrouter-2026}.
9	Concurrent analyses of when and whether to invoke tools~\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026} further presuppose diverse routing signals.
10	The shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources (accuracy, latency, capability matching) and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.
11	But no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing decisions in interactive environments. Without such a decomposition, the field cannot distinguish settings where diverse routing signals justify learned routers from those where a single failure mode dominates and a fixed strategy suffices.
12	
13	We propose a \emph{formal diagnostic framework} for answering this question. The framework comprises (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), a penalty-dependent metric that decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding that holds regardless of how death is penalized. Under ScienceWorld's specific penalty structure ($P{=}100$), the penalty-weighted DAF reaches $90.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$), consistent with the predicted value from observed $\delta{=}0.265$ and $P{=}100$.
14	The internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.
15	Once death episodes are removed, the performance gap collapses to $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$. A per-task consistency check ($r = 0.97$) confirms that the near-perfect correlation is a mathematical consequence of death dominance when $P$ is constant (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}).
16	
17	This framework addresses a methodological gap.
18	The standard approach to generating routing labels, per-step oracle comparison, degenerates under sparse rewards.
19	In ScienceWorld, non-zero reward arrives only at episode termination; 97.4\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical scores regardless of modality, so no step-level signal exists (\S\ref{sec:collection}).
20	Keyword-based heuristics also fail structurally, as ScienceWorld actions lack science-specific terminology (\S\ref{sec:baselines}).
21	Our counterfactual approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compares episode outcomes to generate routing labels (\S\ref{sec:collection}). The framework is applicable to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes, requiring only paired episode outcomes under two modalities.
22	
23	Two cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.
24	First, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation ($N{=}795$ problems, code interpreter as deterministic modality) confirms no routing signal without catastrophic failure: internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, $p = 0.81$).
25	Second, a preliminary cross-model pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (7 task types, $N{=}70$) shows direction-consistent death patterns, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\%$ vs.\ $+26.6\%$ for Qwen; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}).
26	Agent improvement baselines confirm that death dominance is robust: retry yields only marginal reduction, while $10{\times}$ model scaling (72B) and Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} each reduce death rates to ${\sim}$35\% through independent mechanisms, yet neither closes the ${\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).
27	An exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
28	
29	Our contributions are:
30	\begin{enumerate}
31	    \item \textbf{An episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} for decomposing routing signal in interactive environments, with dual penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}) and router training as a diagnostic probe of label informativeness. The framework requires only paired episode outcomes under two modalities with binary catastrophic indicators (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:daf}, \S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
32	
33	    \item \textbf{Evidence that death dominance is robust and routing value is near-degenerate}: 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry, with DAF $= 90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$. The oracle router degenerates to unconditional deterministic computation (96.3\% decision overlap). Retry, $10{\times}$ model scaling, and Reflexion each fail to close the ${\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution. A failure mode taxonomy identifies the root mechanism: 100\% of internal-strategy deaths stem from a single action-space grounding failure (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:agent-improvement}, Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}).
34	
35	    \item \textbf{Cross-environment boundary probes}: MATH ($N{=}795$) confirms the predicted null ($p = 0.81$); ALFWorld ($N{=}135$) identifies information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism; a Llama pilot ($N{=}70$) shows cross-model directional consistency. These are boundary probes of framework applicability, not direct replications (\S\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\ref{sec:cross-model}).
36	\end{enumerate}
37	
38	
39	
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We presented a diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents via episode-level counterfactual analysis. Applied to ScienceWorld (245 episodes, 11 task types), 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry; agent improvement baselines (retry, $10{\times}$ scaling, Reflexion) confirm this is robust. Cross-domain contrasts support framework predictions: MATH shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure; ALFWorld identifies information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism. Replication in permadeath environments (NetHack~\citep{nethack-2020}, Crafter~\citep{crafter-2022}), building on recent LLM agent benchmarks \citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024}, and evaluation with stronger backbones are the most direct open directions.
4	
```

  "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable features. Cross-task-type transfer to physical-science tasks yields chance-level accuracy (49.2\\%, 95\\% CI $[42.4\\%, 55.9\\%]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}), and 7 of 11 task types contain only deterministic-preferred labels, making majority-class collapse a structural consequence of the label distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nFour intervention axes all fail to close the gap to deterministic safety (16.3\\%): Retry ($43.5\\%$ at $N{=}5$), Reflexion ($34.0\\%$ at $N{=}3$, plateauing at $34$--$36.5\\%$), $10{\\times}$ scaling ($34.5\\%$, 72B), and action-space filtering ($29.0\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}). All four leave a ${\\sim}$13--27~pp residual gap. Reflexion and scaling converge to overlapping 95\\% Wilson CIs ($[27.8\\%, 40.8\\%]$ and $[28.0\\%, 41.0\\%]$), suggesting a shared structural bound from the lack of ground-truth state access. Prior tool-routing work \\citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings; in multi-step environments, the death-rate differential ($\\delta = 26.5$~pp) resists all four tested intervention axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \\texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is action-space grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot ground understanding in valid primitives. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\\sim}5\\%$ \\texttt{focus on} rate in both). \\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, the sole zero-death task, uses \\texttt{focus on} more frequently than any death-prone task: non-living objects are the majority class, so indiscriminate submissions rarely trigger death, confirming that lethality is a joint function of action misuse and answer distribution. Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\\% \\to 37.0\\%$ via prompt warning; $\\to 29.0\\%$ via action-space filter), with ironic effects where warnings increased \\texttt{focus on} salience on identification tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy.}\nThe oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions (score improvement: 2.89 points), confirming near-degenerate optimal routing under death dominance.\n\n\\paragraph{Scaling and practical implications.}\nQwen2.5-72B reduces death-prone death rate to $34.5\\%$ yet DAF remains $90.9\\%$; the gap to deterministic persists at ${\\sim}$18~pp. For practitioners: (1)~identify catastrophic failure mechanisms, (2)~estimate death-rate asymmetry via a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot, (3)~if death-asymmetry labels dominate, a fixed deterministic strategy likely matches a learned router. The MATH negative control ($p = 0.81$) validates this diagnostic.\n\n\\paragraph{Framework applicability.}\nThe penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). The framework requires paired modality episodes with binary catastrophic outcomes. NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, are the most direct replication targets; recent benchmarks \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide infrastructure.\n"

          " \\label{sec:discussion}",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}",
          "-The router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly with this signal because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable routing features. A per-class confusion matrix of the 1.7B router reveals that 33\\% of steps in death episodes are mispredicted as ``internal'' (routing to the LLM in precisely the situations where it dies), with internal-class precision of only 0.14. The router's prediction rate is similar across death and non-death episodes, confirming that it cannot detect death risk from step-level features. Cross-task-type transfer reinforces this conclusion: a router trained on life-science tasks achieves balanced accuracy of 49.2\\% on physical-science tasks (95\\% CI $[42.4\\%, 55.9\\%]$, chance level; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}). Leave-one-task-type-out analysis reveals that 7 of 11 task types contain only deterministic-preferred labels, so majority-class collapse (always predict deterministic) is a structural consequence of the label distribution rather than a training failure.",
          "+The router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable features. Cross-task-type transfer to physical-science tasks yields chance-level accuracy (49.2\\%, 95\\% CI $[42.4\\%, 55.9\\%]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}), and 7 of 11 task types contain only deterministic-preferred labels, making majority-class collapse a structural consequence of the label distribution.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}",
          "-Four qualitatively different intervention axes all fail to close the gap to deterministic-level safety (16.3\\%). Retry is nearly ineffective even at $N{=}5$ trials ($43.5\\%$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$ baseline on death-prone tasks, $\\Delta = -4.5$~pp; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose-response}). Reflexion shows a steep initial decline to $34.0\\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p < 0.005$) but plateaus thereafter ($34$--$36.5\\%$ for $N \\geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$). $10{\\times}$ capability scaling reduces death rate to $34.5\\%$ (72B). Action-space filtering that directly blocks the dominant death action during exploration reaches $29.0\\%$ ($\\Delta = -19$~pp), the lowest of all interventions, yet still $12.7$~pp above deterministic. Two independent intervention pathways, verbal self-correction (Reflexion $N{=}3$: $34.0\\%$, 95\\% Wilson CI $[27.8\\%, 40.8\\%]$) and $10{\\times}$ parameter scaling (72B: $34.5\\%$, $[28.0\\%, 41.0\\%]$), converge to overlapping confidence intervals, suggesting a shared structural bound set by the internal strategy's lack of ground-truth state access rather than limitations of either intervention individually. The convergence is a descriptive observation given overlapping CIs, not a precision claim. All four interventions leave the gap to deterministic execution (16.3\\%) at ${\\sim}$13--27~pp (Retry $43.5\\%$, Reflexion $34.0\\%$, 72B $34.5\\%$, filtering $29.0\\%$). \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} exhibits 0\\% death across all conditions, confirming that death risk is task-specific. Compounding state estimation errors across multi-step trajectories create catastrophic failures that single-axis improvements attenuate but do not eliminate. Tree search with environment feedback and frontier models remain untested. Prior tool-routing work \\citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings where individual tool calls are independent; in multi-step interactive environments, the finding is not that death exists but that the death-rate differential ($\\delta = 26.6$~pp) and its downstream effects resist all four tested intervention axes.",
          "+Four intervention axes all fail to close the gap to deterministic safety (16.3\\%): Retry ($43.5\\%$ at $N{=}5$), Reflexion ($34.0\\%$ at $N{=}3$, plateauing at $34$--$36.5\\%$), $10{\\times}$ scaling ($34.5\\%$, 72B), and action-space filtering ($29.0\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}). All four leave a ${\\sim}$13--27~pp residual gap. Reflexion and scaling converge to overlapping 95\\% Wilson CIs ($[27.8\\%, 40.8\\%]$ and $[28.0\\%, 41.0\\%]$), suggesting a shared structural bound from the lack of ground-truth state access. Prior tool-routing work \\citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings; in multi-step environments, the death-rate differential ($\\delta = 26.5$~pp) resists all four tested intervention axes.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}",
          "-All 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \\texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \\texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action; the LLM systematically misuses it as a general interaction command, applying it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items) and thereby submitting incorrect answers that trigger the $-100$ penalty. A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}) identifies action-space grounding failure as the root cause: the majority of dying agents correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives. The dominant failure mode is not comprehension failure but inability to translate task understanding into correct actions. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \\texttt{focus on} rates (${\\sim}5\\%$; Figure~\\ref{fig:death-comparison}); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states rather than different action selection within those states. Among fatal targets, \\texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\\%), followed by \\texttt{inventory} (17.1\\%) and \\texttt{kitchen} (10.5\\%), indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-action-dist}). This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling. \\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, the sole zero-death task, issues \\texttt{focus on} more frequently than any death-prone task (105 vs.\\ ${\\sim}$50 mean), confirming that the action itself is not inherently lethal. Lethality depends on the interaction between \\texttt{focus on} and the task's answer distribution: non-living objects constitute the majority class in ScienceWorld environments, so indiscriminate submissions rarely hit an incorrect answer, whereas for minority-target tasks like \\texttt{find-animal}, random submissions are overwhelmingly fatal. Death dominance thus requires both action-space grounding failure and an answer distribution that penalizes ungrounded submissions.",
          "+All 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \\texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is action-space grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot ground understanding in valid primitives. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\\sim}5\\%$ \\texttt{focus on} rate in both). \\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, the sole zero-death task, uses \\texttt{focus on} more frequently than any death-prone task: non-living objects are the majority class, so indiscriminate submissions rarely trigger death, confirming that lethality is a joint function of action misuse and answer distribution. Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\\% \\to 37.0\\%$ via prompt warning; $\\to 29.0\\%$ via action-space filter), with ironic effects where warnings increased \\texttt{focus on} salience on identification tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Grounding ablation: death is a structural vulnerability.}",
          "-Direct intervention on the dominant death action confirms that the vulnerability is not trivially fixable. An explicit prompt warning (``use \\texttt{focus on} only for final answers'') reduces death rate from 48.0\\% to 37.0\\% ($\\Delta = -11$~pp), though with ironic effects on both identification tasks (\\texttt{identify-life-stages-1}: 35\\%$\\to$50\\%; \\texttt{identify-life-stages-2}: 25\\%$\\to$70\\%), where explicit mention of the dangerous action may have increased its salience, underscoring the unreliability of prompt-based safety interventions. Hard action-space filtering that blocks \\texttt{focus on} during exploration (379 blocks across 200 episodes, zero leaks) reduces it further to 29.0\\%; the agent never persisted in attempting blocked actions (0/379 passthroughs), suggesting \\texttt{focus on} use is a shallow behavioral pattern rather than a deliberate strategy. The gradient $48.0 \\to 37.0 \\to 29.0 \\to 16.3\\%$ (deterministic) shows that each layer captures a portion of the vulnerability but none eliminates it: even with perfect exploration-phase filtering, agents submit \\texttt{focus on} to incorrect objects during the answer window, leaving a 12.7~pp gap to deterministic execution comparable to the residual gaps from Reflexion (34.0\\%) and 72B scaling (34.5\\%). Per-task patterns vary: \\texttt{identify-life-stages} and \\texttt{inclined-plane} approach deterministic-level safety under filtering (${\\sim}$15--20\\% DR), while \\texttt{chemistry-mix} and \\texttt{find-animal} remain at 35--40\\%, indicating that grounding difficulty is task-dependent.",
          "+\\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy.}",
          "+The oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions (score improvement: 2.89 points), confirming near-degenerate optimal routing under death dominance.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and routing value.}",
          "-The oracle router matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions, improving score by only 2.89 points ($-6.58$ vs.\\ $-9.47$). This \\emph{oracle degeneracy} challenges a core assumption of routing research: that learning to switch between modalities yields substantial gains over any fixed strategy. When death dominance holds, the optimal policy is near-degenerate because the dominant signal (avoiding catastrophic failure) uniformly favors deterministic computation. Practitioners should first verify that the oracle margin justifies the complexity of a learned router before investing in routing infrastructure.",
          "+\\paragraph{Scaling and practical implications.}",
          "+Qwen2.5-72B reduces death-prone death rate to $34.5\\%$ yet DAF remains $90.9\\%$; the gap to deterministic persists at ${\\sim}$18~pp. For practitioners: (1)~identify catastrophic failure mechanisms, (2)~estimate death-rate asymmetry via a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot, (3)~if death-asymmetry labels dominate, a fixed deterministic strategy likely matches a learned router. The MATH negative control ($p = 0.81$) validates this diagnostic.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Scaling predictions.}",
          "-Stronger backbones lower death rates but do not eliminate death dominance: Qwen2.5-72B reduces aggregate death from ${\\sim}$51\\% to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($-17$~pp), yet DAF remains $90.9\\%$ (vs.\\ $90.4\\%$ at 7B), and the gap to deterministic (16.3\\%) remains ${\\sim}$18~pp. The Llama pilot ($\\delta = +34.3\\%$) versus Qwen-7B ($\\delta = +26.6\\%$) is consistent in the capability-increase direction. Preliminary evidence (Qwen2.5-0.5B, $N{=}15$) suggests the relationship is non-monotonic: very weak models exhibit low death rates from inability to reach dangerous states. Our results suggest that in environments where one modality triggers irreversible catastrophic failures with severe penalties, death avoidance will dominate routing signal; self-correction mechanisms can selectively reduce death-driven signal but are unlikely to eliminate death dominance.",
          "-",
          "-\\paragraph{When to use deterministic computation.}",
          "-Our framework provides a practical diagnostic sequence for practitioners deploying modular agents. First, identify whether the target environment contains catastrophic failure mechanisms (permadeath, irreversible penalties). Second, estimate the death-rate asymmetry between internal and deterministic strategies using a small pilot (${\\sim}$50 episodes suffice for directional signal). Third, if death-asymmetry labels dominate the routing signal, a fixed deterministic strategy is likely to match or exceed a learned router at substantially lower deployment complexity. The MATH negative control ($p = 0.81$) confirms that this diagnostic correctly identifies environments where routing adds no value.",
          "-",
          "-\\paragraph{DAF and framework applicability.}",
          "-DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}); the penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the more informative primary diagnostic. The framework requires paired episodes under two modalities, binary catastrophic outcomes, and sparse rewards. ScienceWorld satisfies all three; MATH lacks catastrophic outcomes (negative control); ALFWorld lacks them too, and the framework identifies information asymmetry as the alternative mechanism. When $P$ is small relative to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$, DAF degrades (at $P{=}0$, DAF $= 7.5\\%$); the framework is most informative when catastrophic penalties dominate non-death score differences. NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, are the most direct replication targets; recent LLM agent benchmarks in these environments \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide the infrastructure for such evaluation.",
          "+\\paragraph{Framework applicability.}",
          "+The penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). The framework requires paired modality episodes with binary catastrophic outcomes. NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, are the most direct replication targets; recent benchmarks \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide infrastructure."

```text
425	To test whether the death-avoidance finding generalizes beyond Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, we replicate the counterfactual analysis with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on 7 task types ($N{=}70$ episode pairs, 10 episodes per task). The results are strongly consistent with the Qwen findings: 24 episodes (34.3\%) are deterministic-preferred, \textbf{0} (0\%) are internal-preferred, and 46 (65.7\%) show no preference. The internal strategy dies in 58.6\% of episodes versus 24.3\% for deterministic (death-rate differential $+34.3\%$), compared to $+26.6\%$ for Qwen2.5-7B. Direction consistency is perfect: all 7 task types show higher internal than deterministic death rates (7/7, compared to 10/11 for Qwen). The larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\%$ vs.\ $+26.6\%$) and the absence of any internal-preferred episodes (0 vs.\ 9 for Qwen) suggest that Llama-3.1-8B is more vulnerable to hallucination-induced death, amplifying the death-avoidance effect. These results confirm that the death-avoidance dominance is not specific to the Qwen model family.
426	
427	\section{Failure Mode Analysis}
428	\label{app:failure-modes}
429	
430	All 105 internal-strategy deaths (100\%) are triggered by the same action pattern: \texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action (equivalent to ``this is my final answer''); the LLM agent systematically misuses it as a general interaction or exploration command, executing it on irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items). Each such execution submits an incorrect answer, resulting in the $-100$ death penalty. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \texttt{focus on} rates (${\sim}5\%$); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\ 40 for deterministic) reflects how often each strategy enters catastrophic states, not different action selection within those states.
431	
432	Manual inspection identifies four failure categories: (A) no task awareness, where the agent executes \texttt{focus on} without reading the task; (B) task-aware but lacking action grounding, where the agent correctly identifies the objective but cannot map it to valid action primitives; (D) genuine attempts with execution failure; and (E) initial progress wasted by early errors. Category B dominates: agents demonstrate task comprehension but systematically fail to translate it into correct ScienceWorld actions.
433	\label{tab:death-taxonomy}
434	
```

```text
434	
435	\paragraph{Representative examples.}
436	\emph{Category A} (no task awareness): In a \texttt{chemistry-mix} episode, the agent immediately executes \texttt{focus on kitchen}, \texttt{focus on door}, \texttt{focus on inventory} without reading the task, dying on the first \texttt{focus on} call. \emph{Category B} (comprehension without grounding): In a \texttt{lifespan-longest-lived} episode, the agent correctly identifies that it must compare animal lifespans and reasons about which animal lives longest, but then executes \texttt{focus on oak tree} (a non-animal object in the room) instead of navigating to find the target animal, triggering death.
437	
438	\paragraph{Action distribution analysis.}
439	Figure~\ref{fig:death-action-dist} shows the action distribution in death versus non-death episodes for the internal strategy; both are dominated by \texttt{connect} actions, with \texttt{focus on} moderately more frequent in death episodes ($5.4\%$ vs.\ $3.4\%$). Crucially, Figure~\ref{fig:death-comparison} reveals that within death episodes, internal and deterministic strategies exhibit comparable \texttt{focus on} rates (${\sim}5\%$). The survival gap (105 vs.\ 40 deaths) therefore reflects how often each strategy enters catastrophic states, not how it behaves within those states. Among fatal \texttt{focus on} targets, \texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\%), followed by \texttt{inventory} (17.1\%) and \texttt{kitchen} (10.5\%), indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration. Figure~\ref{fig:death-timeline} visualizes the temporal pattern: agents execute long sequences of \texttt{connect} actions before a terminal \texttt{focus on} triggers death, typically in the final 1--3 steps.
440	
441	\begin{figure}[h]
442	\centering
443	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_distribution.pdf}
444	\caption{Action distribution for the internal (LLM) strategy in death ($n{=}105$) vs.\ non-death ($n{=}140$) episodes. Left: full distribution (both dominated by \texttt{connect}). Right: non-\texttt{connect} actions zoomed. \texttt{focus on} is moderately more frequent in death episodes ($5.4\%$ vs.\ $3.4\%$), while non-death episodes show higher rates of observation and object interaction.}
445	\label{fig:death-action-dist}
446	\end{figure}
447	
448	\begin{figure}[h]
449	\centering
450	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_timeline.pdf}
451	\caption{Step-by-step action sequences for 6 representative death episodes. The fatal \texttt{focus on} action (marked with arrow) consistently appears in the final steps after long \texttt{connect} sequences, illustrating the ``connect loop $\to$ fatal focus'' pattern. Examples are drawn from the full 30-task dataset to maximize task-type diversity; the pattern is identical in the 11-task analysis subset.}
452	\label{fig:death-timeline}
453	\end{figure}
454	
455	\begin{figure}[h]
456	\centering
457	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_comparison.pdf}
458	\caption{Non-\texttt{connect} action distribution comparison between internal (left) and deterministic (right) strategies. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \texttt{focus on} rates (${\sim}5\%$); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different action selection within those states.}
459	\label{fig:death-comparison}
460	\end{figure}
461	
462	\section{Ablation Studies}
463	\label{app:ablations}
```

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/main.tex",
  "old_string": "\\section{Ablation Studies}\n\\label{app:ablations}",
  "new_string": "\\section{Grounding Ablation}\n\\label{app:grounding-ablation}\n\nWe test whether directly intervening on the dominant death action (\\texttt{focus on}) eliminates death vulnerability on the 10 death-prone tasks (baseline 48.0\\%, $n{=}200$).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt warning.} Adding an explicit system prompt (``use \\texttt{focus on} only for final answers'') reduces aggregate death rate to 37.0\\% ($\\Delta = -11$~pp). Both identification tasks exhibit ironic effects (\\texttt{identify-life-stages-1}: 35\\%$\\to$50\\%; \\texttt{identify-life-stages-2}: 25\\%$\\to$70\\%), where explicit mention of the dangerous action may have increased its salience.\n\n\\paragraph{Action-space filtering.} Blocking \\texttt{focus on} during the exploration phase yields 379 blocks across 200 episodes with zero leaks and zero passthroughs (the agent never persisted in retrying blocked actions), reducing death rate to 29.0\\% ($\\Delta = -19$~pp). The zero-passthrough rate suggests \\texttt{focus on} use is a shallow behavioral pattern rather than a deliberate strategy.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-task variation.} Under filtering, \\texttt{identify-life-stages} and \\texttt{inclined-plane} approach deterministic-level safety (${\\sim}$15--20\\% DR), while \\texttt{chemistry-mix} and \\texttt{find-animal} remain at 35--40\\%. Residual deaths occur during the answer-submission window, where the filter is inactive and agents submit \\texttt{focus on} to incorrect objects.\n\n\\section{Ablation Studies}\n\\label{app:ablations}"
}
```

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Seed robustness.}\nA full replication with an independent environment seed (seed$=$123, $N{=}220$, 11 tasks, strictly 20 episodes per task; the primary seed$=$42 analysis uses $N{=}245$ with 20--40 episodes per task) yields DAF $= 89.7\\%$, within 0.7~pp of the primary analysis ($90.4\\%$). The death-asymmetry label fraction rises from $78.9\\%$ (seed$=$42) to $86.5\\%$ (seed$=$123), and the non-death score differential is virtually identical ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.83$ vs.\\ $2.84$), with per-task death-rate correlation $r = 0.94$. Internal and deterministic death rates shift modestly ($45.5\\%$ vs.\\ $42.9\\%$ and $20.9\\%$ vs.\\ $16.3\\%$, respectively), but the asymmetry and its downstream effect on routing signal are stable across seeds. \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} exhibits zero deaths under both seeds, remaining the sole task with score-based (rather than death-based) routing signal, further confirming that the remaining 10 tasks' signal derives almost entirely from death asymmetry. Death dominance is not a seed artifact.",

The death-asymmetry label fraction rises from $78.9\\%$ (seed$=$42) to $86.5\\%$ (seed$=$123), and the non-death score differential is virtually identical ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.83$ vs.\\ $2.84$), with per-task death-rate correlation $r = 0.94$. {
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance. Retry gives the agent three attempts per episode without self-correction; Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} adds verbal self-reflection between trials (Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry yields only marginal reduction on death-prone tasks ($43.5\\%$ at $N{=}5$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$ baseline, $\\Delta = -4.5$~pp; see dose-response analysis below). Reflexion reduces death to 31.4\\% by rescuing 29.3\\% of first-trial deaths (27 of 92), but the gap to deterministic (16.3\\%) remains 15.1~pp. Trial~1 death rate under Reflexion (41.8\\%) matches the single-trial baseline; the reduction comes from cross-trial self-correction, not from the Reflexion prompt itself. Trial~3 adds minimal rescue beyond Trial~2 (net $+4$). Scaling from 7B to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$ parameters) moderately reduces aggregate death rate from ${\\sim}$51\\% to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp) across death-prone tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement}), but does not approach deterministic-level safety (16.3\\%). Task-level variation ranges from $+5$~pp (chemistry-mix, where 72B performs slightly worse) to $-31$~pp (lifespan-longest-lived). This variation suggests that scaling yields large reductions in biological-reasoning tasks ($-15$ to $-31$~pp) but provides no benefit for chemical state tracking (chemistry-mix: $+5$~pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}). The 31.4\\% in Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement} covers all 11 tasks; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\\sim}$35\\%, the figure reported in the Abstract. Recomputing DAF with 72B as the internal strategy ($N{=}220$) yields $90.9\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[83.9\\%, 97.3\\%]$), nearly identical to the 7B value ($90.4\\%$); the death-asymmetry label fraction rises from $78.9\\%$ (7B) to $84.3\\%$ (72B). On death-prone tasks ($N{=}200$), DAF reaches $99.5\\%$ ($[98.0\\%, 100.2\\%]$) with $95.9\\%$ of labels driven by death asymmetry. A matched 10-task comparison confirms scale-independence: 8 of 10 death-prone tasks maintain DAF $= 100\\%$ at both 7B and 72B; episode-weighted DAF declines only from $100\\%$ to $97.5\\%$. No task shifts from death-dominated to performance-dominated at $10{\\times}$ scale. Scaling lowers death rate but leaves death dominance structurally intact.",
  "new_string": "We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$ attempts without self-correction) yields marginal reduction on death-prone tasks ($43.5\\%$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$ baseline, $\\Delta = -4.5$~pp). Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} (verbal self-reflection between trials) reduces death to 31.4\\% across all 11 tasks by rescuing 29.3\\% of first-trial deaths, but the gap to deterministic remains 15.1~pp; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\\sim}$35\\%. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$ parameters) reduces death-prone rate to $34.5\\%$ with task-level variation from $+5$~pp (chemistry-mix) to $-31$~pp (lifespan-longest-lived; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}). Recomputing DAF with 72B yields $90.9\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[83.9\\%, 97.3\\%]$), nearly identical to 7B ($90.4\\%$); scaling lowers death rate but leaves death dominance structurally intact."
}

Retry ($N{=}5$ attempts without self-correction) yields marginal reduction on death-prone tasks ($43.5\\%$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$ baseline, $\\Delta = -4.5$~pp). Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} (verbal self-reflection between trials) reduces death to 31.4\\% across all 11 tasks by rescuing 29.3\\% of first-trial deaths, but the gap to deterministic remains 15.1~pp; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\\sim}$35\\%. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$ parameters) reduces death-prone rate to $34.5\\%$ with task-level variation from $+5$~pp (chemistry-mix) to $-31$~pp (lifespan-longest-lived; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}). Recomputing DAF with 72B yields $90.9\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[83.9\\%, 97.3\\%]$), nearly identical to 7B ($90.4\\%$); scaling lowers death rate but leaves death dominance structurally intact.",

          " To characterize the marginal value of additional trials, we extend both Retry and Reflexion to $N{=}5$ trials on the 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$ per condition; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat: death rate decreases from $48.0\\%$ ($N{=}1$) to $43.5\\%$ ($N{=}5$), a $4.5$~pp reduction across five trials. That Reflexion at $N{=}3$ ($34.0\\%$) converges to nearly the same floor as $10{\\times}$ capability scaling ($34.5\\%$, 72B) suggests a shared structural bound: both interventions attenuate death but cannot close the ${\\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution ($16.3\\%$). The contrast illuminates the mechanism: Retry's per-trial conditional death rate remains constant at ${\\sim}48\\%$, confirming that the agent does not learn between blind retries. ```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The primary bound on performance is the oracle margin: only 2.89 points separate the oracle ($-6.58$) from always-deterministic ($-9.47$), capping routing upside regardless of router quality. A secondary confound is that ${\\sim}27.7\\%$ of step-level labels are misaligned with their episode-level assignment, further limiting step-level learnability. These two factors are logically independent: a narrow oracle margin limits routing value even with perfectly accurate labels, while mislabeling degrades learning even if the margin were large. In this setting, the oracle margin is the binding constraint: the 2.89-point ceiling means routing can recover at most modest gains regardless of label quality. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA fine-tuned; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) is trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with identical 4-seed configurations. Step-level balanced accuracy is flat across scales ($56.4 \\pm 3.5\\%$, $58.2 \\pm 4.0\\%$, $59.2 \\pm 3.5\\%$; all within seed variance); step-level discrimination is not capacity-sensitive. At episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline ($59.0 \\pm 1.9\\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}), while 0.6B ($50.7 \\pm 2.6\\%$) and 1.7B ($43.1 \\pm 1.0\\%$) remain at or below chance. The 1.7B dip is robust across seeds (range $41.7$--$44.4\\%$). We hypothesize that the 1.7B model has sufficient capacity to memorize death-correlated patterns in the training set but insufficient capacity to learn a generalizable routing policy, resulting in spurious anti-correlation; future work should examine per-class routing decisions to test this hypothesis. This non-monotonic pattern, combined with universally low internal recall, reinforces that learned routing does not scale predictably with capacity at current data scales. Unlike single-step tool-calling, the multi-step nature of this routing problem (10--50 actions with compounding state uncertainty) creates inherent label noise between step-level decisions and episode-level outcomes.",
  "new_string": "We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside; ${\\sim}27.7\\%$ of step-level labels are misaligned with episode-level assignments, further limiting learnability. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with 4-seed configurations shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$, within seed variance). At episode level, only 4B exceeds always-deterministic ($59.0 \\pm 1.9\\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at or below chance. The multi-step nature of routing (10--50 actions with compounding state uncertainty) creates inherent label noise between step-level decisions and episode-level outcomes."
}

    "oldString": "We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The primary bound on performance is the oracle margin: only 2.89 points separate the oracle ($-6.58$) from always-deterministic ($-9.47$), capping routing upside regardless of router quality. A secondary confound is that ${\\sim}27.7\\%$ of step-level labels are misaligned with their episode-level assignment, further limiting step-level learnability. These two factors are logically independent: a narrow oracle margin limits routing value even with perfectly accurate labels, while mislabeling degrades learning even if the margin were large. In this setting, the oracle margin is the binding constraint: the 2.89-point ceiling means routing can recover at most modest gains regardless of label quality. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA fine-tuned; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) is trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with identical 4-seed configurations. Step-level balanced accuracy is flat across scales ($56.4 \\pm 3.5\\%$, $58.2 \\pm 4.0\\%$, $59.2 \\pm 3.5\\%$; all within seed variance); step-level discrimination is not capacity-sensitive. At episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline ($59.0 \\pm 1.9\\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}), while 0.6B ($50.7 \\pm 2.6\\%$) and 1.7B ($43.1 \\pm 1.0\\%$) remain at or below chance. The 1.7B dip is robust across seeds (range $41.7$--$44.4\\%$). We hypothesize that the 1.7B model has sufficient capacity to memorize death-correlated patterns in the training set but insufficient capacity to learn a generalizable routing policy, resulting in spurious anti-correlation; future work should examine per-class routing decisions to test this hypothesis. This non-monotonic pattern, combined with universally low internal recall, reinforces that learned routing does not scale predictably with capacity at current data scales. Unlike single-step tool-calling, the multi-step nature of this routing problem (10--50 actions with compounding state uncertainty) creates inherent label noise between step-level decisions and episode-level outcomes.",

          " \\label{sec:router-diagnostic}",
          " ",
          "-We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The primary bound on performance is the oracle margin: only 2.89 points separate the oracle ($-6.58$) from always-deterministic ($-9.47$), capping routing upside regardless of router quality. A secondary confound is that ${\\sim}27.7\\%$ of step-level labels are misaligned with their episode-level assignment, further limiting step-level learnability. These two factors are logically independent: a narrow oracle margin limits routing value even with perfectly accurate labels, while mislabeling degrades learning even if the margin were large. In this setting, the oracle margin is the binding constraint: the 2.89-point ceiling means routing can recover at most modest gains regardless of label quality. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA fine-tuned; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) is trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with identical 4-seed configurations. Step-level balanced accuracy is flat across scales ($56.4 \\pm 3.5\\%$, $58.2 \\pm 4.0\\%$, $59.2 \\pm 3.5\\%$; all within seed variance); step-level discrimination is not capacity-sensitive. At episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline ($59.0 \\pm 1.9\\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}), while 0.6B ($50.7 \\pm 2.6\\%$) and 1.7B ($43.1 \\pm 1.0\\%$) remain at or below chance. The 1.7B dip is robust across seeds (range $41.7$--$44.4\\%$). We hypothesize that the 1.7B model has sufficient capacity to memorize death-correlated patterns in the training set but insufficient capacity to learn a generalizable routing policy, resulting in spurious anti-correlation; future work should examine per-class routing decisions to test this hypothesis. This non-monotonic pattern, combined with universally low internal recall, reinforces that learned routing does not scale predictably with capacity at current data scales. Unlike single-step tool-calling, the multi-step nature of this routing problem (10--50 actions with compounding state uncertainty) creates inherent label noise between step-level decisions and episode-level outcomes.",

          " ",
          " \\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} Deployed in the full ScienceWorld loop ($N{=}245$), the 4B router scores $-12.03$ (95\\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$; overlapping CIs). Paraphrase-based augmentation ($25\\%$--$800\\%$, synthetic oversampling without new environment interactions) provides no improvement (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}); augmentation inflates the majority class without generating novel internal-preferred patterns (only 9 of 245 base episodes are internal-preferred), so real-data collection may be necessary but the $3.7\\%$ base rate makes it expensive. The agent improvement results (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) reinforce this ceiling: even Reflexion-level intervention leaves a 15.1~pp death gap to deterministic. Failure mode, augmentation, and ablation details in Appendices~\\ref{app:failure-modes}--\\ref{app:ablations}."

  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
  "old_string": "\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment $\\mathcal{E}$ with state space $\\mathcal{S}$, observation space $\\mathcal{O}$, and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent follows a policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ that maps observation histories to actions. At each step $t$, the agent receives observation $o_t$, selects action $a_t = \\pi(o_1, \\ldots, o_t)$, and the environment transitions to state $s_{t+1}$.\n\nWe define a modality set $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$ governing how the agent processes state transitions:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Internal} ($m = {\\tt int}$): The agent uses its own generation to predict intermediate observations. The LLM interleaves chain-of-thought reasoning with environment actions following the ReAct paradigm \\cite{react-2023}, but prediction errors compound across steps.\n    \\item \\textbf{Deterministic} ($m = {\\tt det}$): The agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step, equivalent to access to a perfect dynamics model. Action selection remains with the LLM; only the state-transition prediction is replaced.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent We restrict $\\mathcal{M}$ to two modalities. A reasoning-depth ablation (chain-of-thought vs.\\ direct-answer, $n{=}120$, 6 task types) found no significant difference (Mann--Whitney $p > 0.26$, near-identical death rates); details in Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}.\n\nA \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which modality to use at each step. At each step $t$, the agent receives observation $o_t$, selects action $a_t = \\pi(o_1, \\ldots, o_t)$, and the environment transitions to state $s_{t+1}$.\n\nWe define a modality set $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$ governing how the agent processes state transitions:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Internal} ($m = {\\tt int}$): The agent uses its own generation to predict intermediate observations. We define two computation modalities $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\cite{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), where the agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which modality to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$. A reasoning-depth ablation ($n{=}120$) found no significant difference ($p > 0.26$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically (Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment $\\mathcal{E}$ with state space $\\mathcal{S}$, observation space $\\mathcal{O}$, and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent follows a policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ that maps observation histories to actions. At each step $t$, the agent receives observation $o_t$, selects action $a_t = \\pi(o_1, \\ldots, o_t)$, and the environment transitions to state $s_{t+1}$.\n\nWe define a modality set $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$ governing how the agent processes state transitions:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Internal} ($m = {\\tt int}$): The agent uses its own generation to predict intermediate observations. The LLM interleaves chain-of-thought reasoning with environment actions following the ReAct paradigm \\cite{react-2023}, but prediction errors compound across steps.\n    \\item \\textbf{Deterministic} ($m = {\\tt det}$): The agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step, equivalent to access to a perfect dynamics model. Action selection remains with the LLM; only the state-transition prediction is replaced.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent We restrict $\\mathcal{M}$ to two modalities. A reasoning-depth ablation (chain-of-thought vs.\\ direct-answer, $n{=}120$, 6 task types) found no significant difference (Mann--Whitney $p > 0.26$, near-identical death rates); details in Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}.\n\nA \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which modality to use at each step. The goal of modality routing is to learn $\\rho$ that maximizes expected episode return. Our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn such a router.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling assigns routing labels by comparing single-step outcomes under each computation modality. Under sparse terminal rewards, this approach degenerates: on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores regardless of modality, yielding no routing signal. We instead perform episode-level counterfactual analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and modality $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying modality $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the modality choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM) on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two modalities can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score to reflect the asymmetric cost of catastrophic failure in interactive environments. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. The full procedure is given in Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}\n\\label{alg:labeling}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, modalities $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$\n\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}\n    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$\n    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = 0$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{both dead}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt det} > s_{\\tt int}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt int} > s_{\\tt det}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $\\ell$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\nThe algorithm prioritizes survival over score: death asymmetry (lines 5--8) determines preference before score comparison (lines 11--14). Label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. The full gap decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation holds exactly when the surviving strategy scores zero in all death-asymmetry episodes; in our data, the residual is small ($29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$, or $0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction as complementary metrics.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nA complementary question is whether death-avoidance signal is driven by a few outlier task types or distributed systematically. We quantify this via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI) based on the Gini coefficient over per-task routing signal contributions (formal definition in Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). SCI $\\in [0, 1]$: values near 0 indicate uniform distribution (systematic phenomenon); values near 1 indicate concentration in a single type (isolated phenomenon). Results are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n",

    "oldString": "\\paragraph{Safe RL.}\n\\citet{garcia2015comprehensive} survey safe RL methods; \\citet{tamar2015cvar} optimize CVaR for risk-sensitive policies; the constrained MDP framework \\citep{altman1999cmdp}, Constrained Policy Optimization \\citep{achiam2017cpo}, and \\citet{crpo-2021} provide formal foundations for enforcing safety constraints. SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection specifically for safety guardrails. Our contribution is complementary: we provide a diagnostic that shows modality routing already functions as an implicit safety mechanism in environments with catastrophic failure.",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Safe RL.}\nSafe RL methods \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,tamar2015cvar,achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021} enforce constraints in MDPs; SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails. Our diagnostic shows modality routing already functions as an implicit safety mechanism in environments with catastrophic failure.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}\nA growing body of work augments language models with external tools \\citep{tool-learning-survey-2024}.\nToolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} teaches LLMs to insert API calls via self-supervised loss filtering; ToolkenGPT \\cite{toolkengpt-2023} represents tools as special tokens; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} composes heterogeneous modules per subtask; ReTool \\cite{retool-2025} applies RL to discover when code execution is preferable to chain-of-thought; ToolACE \\cite{toolace-2025} optimizes function-calling accuracy through synthetic data curation.\nThese focus on \\emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \\emph{what signal drives the routing decision?}\nRecent concurrent work sharpens this question: \\citet{tool-overuse-illusion-2026} identify a cognitive-illusion effect where LLMs default to external tools even when internal knowledge suffices, finding that tool overuse correlates with model uncertainty rather than task difficulty. \\citet{when2tool-2026} show tool-call decisions are encoded in hidden states before reasoning, \\citet{tool-use-tax-2026} quantify a ``tool-use tax,'' and \\citet{to-call-or-not-2026} propose a necessity--utility--affordability framework. Our decomposition provides environment-grounded evidence: in ScienceWorld, 78.9\\% of routing labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.\n\n\\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}\nCogRouter \\cite{cogrouter-2026} adapts reasoning depth per step, ARES \\cite{ares-2026} selects among reasoning effort levels, Mixture-of-Recursions \\cite{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths, and MTRouter \\cite{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn routing.\nRouteLLM \\citep{routellm-2024} learns routers from preference data to select between strong and weak models.\nThese optimize \\emph{how} to route; we reveal \\emph{why} routing helps: in interactive environments, the dominant signal is catastrophic-failure avoidance.\n\n\\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation in RL.}\nPrior work generates counterfactual explanations for RL agents by perturbing individual actions within a fixed policy \\citep{olson2021counterfactual, gajcin2023counterfactual, amitai2024explaining}.\nOur approach differs in both purpose and granularity: we execute two complete episodes under different routing strategies, producing prospective paired comparisons that isolate routing signal \\emph{before} any router is trained.\n\n\\paragraph{LLM Agent Environments and Reasoning.}\nScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; Inner Monologue \\cite{huang2022inner} grounds LLM planning in embodied feedback; Voyager \\cite{wang2023voyager} demonstrates open-ended LLM agents; ALFWorld \\cite{alfworld-2021} provides a complementary household environment; AgentBench \\cite{agentbench-2024} offers systematic evaluation. \\citet{zhou2024webarena} document common failure modes (premature termination, hallucinated actions) in web-based agents, consistent with our finding that state hallucination is the primary internal-strategy failure mechanism. ReAct \\cite{react-2023} serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \\cite{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection; LATS \\cite{lats-2024} adds tree search. SelfCheckGPT \\cite{selfcheckgpt-2023} detects unreliable LLM outputs; our work shows that in interactive environments, such unreliability manifests as catastrophic death.\n\n\\paragraph{Safe RL.}\n\\citet{garcia2015comprehensive} survey safe RL methods; \\citet{tamar2015cvar} optimize CVaR for risk-sensitive policies; the constrained MDP framework \\citep{altman1999cmdp}, Constrained Policy Optimization \\citep{achiam2017cpo}, and \\citet{crpo-2021} provide formal foundations for enforcing safety constraints. SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection specifically for safety guardrails. Our contribution is complementary: we provide a diagnostic that shows modality routing already functions as an implicit safety mechanism in environments with catastrophic failure.\n",

ReAct \\cite{react-2023} serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \\cite{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection; LATS \\cite{lats-2024} adds tree search. SelfCheckGPT \\cite{selfcheckgpt-2023} detects unreliable LLM outputs; our work shows that in interactive environments, such unreliability manifests as catastrophic death.",

          " Our internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}. Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death rate from 42.9\\% to 31.4\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), but more aggressive self-correction or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\\citep{tot-2023} may further reduce internal death rates. Episode-level labels assign a single routing preference to entire episodes, which may be suboptimal for individual steps (particularly those preceding death events). The mislabeling fraction (episodes where the episode-level label disagrees with what step-level oracle labels would assign) is approximately 27.7\\%, concentrated in death-asymmetry episodes where early steps may have favored the eventually-dying strategy. Training on pure-strategy trajectories also introduces distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Statistical limitations.}",
          "-Episodes within the same task type are not fully independent because they share environment dynamics, though they use different random seeds. Cluster bootstrap with $K{=}11$ task-type clusters yields wider CIs ($[73.3\\%, 100\\%]$); with $K < 40$ clusters, these should be interpreted cautiously~\\citep{cameron-2008,webb-2023}. A wild cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$, $p < 0.001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}) and 100\\% directional consistency across all 28 death-prone tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task-ci}) mitigate this concern.",
          "+Episodes within the same task type are not fully independent because they share environment dynamics, though they use different random seeds. Cluster bootstrap with $K{=}11$ task-type clusters yields wider CIs ($[73.0\\%, 100\\%]$); with $K < 40$ clusters, these should be interpreted cautiously~\\citep{cameron-2008,webb-2023}. A wild cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$, $p < 0.001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}) and 100\\% directional consistency across all 28 death-prone tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task-ci}) mitigate this concern.",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nA Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation with 30 task types, faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.\nToolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.\nMore recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \\cite{mtrouter-2026}.\nConcurrent analyses of when and whether to invoke tools~\\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026} further presuppose diverse routing signals.\nThe shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources (accuracy, latency, capability matching) and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.\nBut no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing decisions in interactive environments. Without such a decomposition, the field cannot distinguish settings where diverse routing signals justify learned routers from those where a single failure mode dominates and a fixed strategy suffices.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{formal diagnostic framework} for answering this question. The framework comprises (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), a penalty-dependent metric that decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding that holds regardless of how death is penalized. Under ScienceWorld's specific penalty structure ($P{=}100$), the penalty-weighted DAF reaches $90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$), consistent with the predicted value from observed $\\delta{=}0.265$ and $P{=}100$.\nThe internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.\nOnce death episodes are removed, the performance gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$. A per-task consistency check ($r = 0.97$) confirms that the near-perfect correlation is a mathematical consequence of death dominance when $P$ is constant (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n\nThis framework addresses a methodological gap.\nThe standard approach to generating routing labels, per-step oracle comparison, degenerates under sparse rewards.\nIn ScienceWorld, non-zero reward arrives only at episode termination; 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical scores regardless of modality, so no step-level signal exists (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\nKeyword-based heuristics also fail structurally, as ScienceWorld actions lack science-specific terminology (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}).\nOur counterfactual approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compares episode outcomes to generate routing labels (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}). The framework is applicable to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes, requiring only paired episode outcomes under two modalities.\n\nTwo cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.\nFirst, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation ($N{=}795$ problems, code interpreter as deterministic modality) confirms no routing signal without catastrophic failure: internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$).\nSecond, a preliminary cross-model pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (7 task types, $N{=}70$) shows direction-consistent death patterns, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.6\\%$ for Qwen; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}).\nAgent improvement baselines confirm that death dominance is robust: retry yields only marginal reduction, while $10{\\times}$ model scaling (72B) and Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} each reduce death rates to ${\\sim}$35\\% through independent mechanisms, yet neither closes the ${\\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{An episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} for decomposing routing signal in interactive environments, with dual penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}) and router training as a diagnostic probe of label informativeness. The framework requires only paired episode outcomes under two modalities with binary catastrophic indicators (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf}, \\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Evidence that death dominance is robust and routing value is near-degenerate}: 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry, with DAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$. The oracle router degenerates to unconditional deterministic computation (96.3\\% decision overlap). Retry, $10{\\times}$ model scaling, and Reflexion each fail to close the ${\\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution. A failure mode taxonomy identifies the root mechanism: 100\\% of internal-strategy deaths stem from a single action-space grounding failure (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}, Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-environment boundary probes}: MATH ($N{=}795$) confirms the predicted null ($p = 0.81$); ALFWorld ($N{=}135$) identifies information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism; a Llama pilot ($N{=}70$) shows cross-model directional consistency. These are boundary probes of framework applicability, not direct replications (\\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011AucP5fRkg9Z7MVqQfAPij`</summary>

```text
424	
425	To test whether the death-avoidance finding generalizes beyond Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, we replicate the counterfactual analysis with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on 7 task types ($N{=}70$ episode pairs, 10 episodes per task). The results are strongly consistent with the Qwen findings: 24 episodes (34.3\%) are deterministic-preferred, \textbf{0} (0\%) are internal-preferred, and 46 (65.7\%) show no preference. The internal strategy dies in 58.6\% of episodes versus 24.3\% for deterministic (death-rate differential $+34.3\%$), compared to $+26.6\%$ for Qwen2.5-7B. Direction consistency is perfect: all 7 task types show higher internal than deterministic death rates (7/7, compared to 10/11 for Qwen). The larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\%$ vs.\ $+26.6\%$) and the absence of any internal-preferred episodes (0 vs.\ 9 for Qwen) suggest that Llama-3.1-8B is more vulnerable to hallucination-induced death, amplifying the death-avoidance effect. These results confirm that the death-avoidance dominance is not specific to the Qwen model family.
426	
```

          " \\label{app:cross-model}",
          " ",
          "-To test whether the death-avoidance finding generalizes beyond Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, we replicate the counterfactual analysis with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on 7 task types ($N{=}70$ episode pairs, 10 episodes per task). The results are strongly consistent with the Qwen findings: 24 episodes (34.3\\%) are deterministic-preferred, \\textbf{0} (0\\%) are internal-preferred, and 46 (65.7\\%) show no preference. The internal strategy dies in 58.6\\% of episodes versus 24.3\\% for deterministic (death-rate differential $+34.3\\%$), compared to $+26.6\\%$ for Qwen2.5-7B. Direction consistency is perfect: all 7 task types show higher internal than deterministic death rates (7/7, compared to 10/11 for Qwen). The larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.6\\%$) and the absence of any internal-preferred episodes (0 vs.\\ 9 for Qwen) suggest that Llama-3.1-8B is more vulnerable to hallucination-induced death, amplifying the death-avoidance effect. These results confirm that the death-avoidance dominance is not specific to the Qwen model family.",
