```text
174:Episodes within the same task type share environment dynamics, raising the concern that the effective sample size is closer to $K$ (task types) than $N$ (episodes). We address this with cluster-robust inference treating each task type as a cluster ($K{=}11$, all 11 included task types, 245 episodes).
193:\noindent All methods confirm significance with $p < 0.01$, and the 95\% CI $[0.73, 1.00]$ excludes zero by a wide margin. The $K{=}11$ analysis uses the same clusters as the main analysis, providing a direct robustness check on the primary results.
237:Table~\ref{tab:dose-response-detail} reports per-task death rates for Reflexion at $N{=}3$, 4, and 5 on the 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}20$ episodes per task). Per-task variance is high (up to 25\,pp range for \texttt{measure-melting-point}), but the aggregate smooths to a 2.5\,pp band, confirming that the plateau conclusion is robust at the aggregate level despite task-level noise. \texttt{freeze} is consistently the hardest task (50--60\% DR across all $N$).
344:\paragraph{Data scaling ablation.} To test whether the episode-level generalization failure stems from data scarcity, we vary training set size from 25\% to 800\% of the original via paraphrase-based data augmentation (synonym substitution + template variation on step observations), holding the episode-level validation set fixed. Table~\ref{tab:data-scaling} reports the results.
372:[Omitted long matching line]
420:[Omitted long matching line]
425:[Omitted long matching line]
432:[Omitted long matching line]
436:[Omitted long matching line]
439:[Omitted long matching line]
451:\caption{Step-by-step action sequences for 6 representative death episodes. The fatal \texttt{focus on} action (marked with arrow) consistently appears in the final steps after long \texttt{connect} sequences, illustrating the ``connect loop $\to$ fatal focus'' pattern. Examples are drawn from the full 30-task dataset to maximize task-type diversity; the pattern is identical in the 11-task analysis subset.}
469:\paragraph{Action-space filtering.} Blocking \texttt{focus on} during the exploration phase yields 379 blocks across 200 episodes with zero leaks and zero passthroughs (the agent never persisted in retrying blocked actions), reducing death rate to 29.0\% ($\Delta = -19$~pp). The zero-passthrough rate suggests \texttt{focus on} use is a shallow behavioral pattern rather than a deliberate strategy.
471:\paragraph{Per-task variation.} Under filtering, \texttt{identify-life-stages} and \texttt{inclined-plane} approach deterministic-level safety (${\sim}$15--20\% DR), while \texttt{chemistry-mix} and \texttt{find-animal} remain at 35--40\%. Residual deaths occur during the answer-submission window, where the filter is inactive and agents submit \texttt{focus on} to incorrect objects.
498:\paragraph{Generative router.} A generative routing model trained with explicit routing labels achieves balanced accuracy of 46.7\%, confirming that the routing bottleneck is signal-limited, not architecture-limited.
```

```text
22	
23	\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis.}
24	Episode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\% CI $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$ for DAF; per-task CIs confirm $\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 types. Cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$, resampling task types) yields a wider $[73.0\%, 100\%]$; with $K < 40$ this should be interpreted cautiously~\citep{cameron-2008}. A wild cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$ task clusters, $B{=}9{,}999$) confirms significance at the cluster level ($p < 0.001$, Cohen's $d = 3.02$, 95\% CI $[0.73, 1.00]$; Appendix~\ref{app:wild-cluster}). Both intervals exclude 50\%, confirming that death avoidance accounts for a majority of routing signal under resampling. A leave-one-task-out analysis confirms DAF stability: removing any single death-prone task changes DAF by at most 3.0\,pp (range: 75.9\%--78.9\%; Appendix~\ref{app:loto-daf}). Across all 28 death-prone tasks (included and excluded), internal DR exceeds deterministic DR in every case (100\% directional consistency), with pooled $\Delta = +37.3\%$ (95\% CI: $[29.7\%, 44.4\%]$); 15 of 28 are individually significant, with the 13 non-significant cases entirely explained by small sample size ($n{=}5$; Appendix~\ref{app:per-task-ci}). An 8-seed cross-validation of the router ($47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy, all seeds below chance) confirms that the failure to learn routing generalizes across data splits (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}).
```

</details>

```json
          " The Signal Concentration Index (SCI $= 0.2147$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) confirms low concentration: the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\\%$ of total death-rate differential. One outlier, \\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, has zero deaths ($\\Delta = +9.58$ from non-death scores alone); all 10 remaining types exhibit death-asymmetry-driven routing labels.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis.}",
          "-Episode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\\% CI $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$ for DAF; per-task CIs confirm $\\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 types. Cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$, resampling task types) yields a wider $[73.0\\%, 100\\%]$; with $K < 40$ this should be interpreted cautiously~\\citep{cameron-2008}. A wild cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$ task clusters, $B{=}9{,}999$) confirms significance at the cluster level ($p < 0.001$, Cohen's $d = 3.02$, 95\\% CI $[0.73, 1.00]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}). A leave-one-task-out analysis confirms DAF stability: removing any single death-prone task changes DAF by at most 3.0\\,pp (range: 75.9\\%--78.9\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:loto-daf}). Across all 28 death-prone tasks (included and excluded), internal DR exceeds deterministic DR in every case (100\\% directional consistency), with pooled $\\Delta = +37.3\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[29.7\\%, 44.4\\%]$); 15 of 28 are individually significant, with the 13 non-significant cases entirely explained by small sample size ($n{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task-ci}). ### Tool result
```

_source line 1832 · 2026-05-19T01:54:48.024Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017354ZYmJ1ckoXjAC6tFXg1`</summary>

```text
430	All 105 internal-strategy deaths (100\%) are triggered by the same action pattern: \texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action (equivalent to ``this is my final answer''); the LLM agent systematically misuses it as a general interaction or exploration command, executing it on irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items). Each such execution submits an incorrect answer, resulting in the $-100$ death penalty. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \texttt{focus on} rates (${\sim}5\%$); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\ 40 for deterministic) reflects how often each strategy enters catastrophic states, not different action selection within those states.
431	
432	Manual inspection identifies four failure categories: (A) no task awareness, where the agent executes \texttt{focus on} without reading the task; (B) task-aware but lacking action grounding, where the agent correctly identifies the objective but cannot map it to valid action primitives; (D) genuine attempts with execution failure; and (E) initial progress wasted by early errors. Category B dominates: agents demonstrate task comprehension but systematically fail to translate it into correct ScienceWorld actions.
433	\label{tab:death-taxonomy}
434	
```

</details>

```json
          " \\label{app:failure-modes}",
          " ",
          "-All 105 internal-strategy deaths (100\\%) are triggered by the same action pattern: \\texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \\texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action (equivalent to ``this is my final answer''); the LLM agent systematically misuses it as a general interaction or exploration command, executing it on irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items).           "+All 105 internal-strategy deaths (100\\%) are triggered by the same action pattern: \\texttt{focus on [object]}. Each such execution submits an incorrect answer and triggers the $-100$ death penalty. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \\texttt{focus on} rates (${\\sim}5\\%$); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40 for deterministic) reflects how often each strategy enters catastrophic states, not different action selection within those states.",
          " ",
          " Manual inspection identifies four failure categories: (A) no task awareness, where the agent executes \\texttt{focus on} without reading the task; (B) task-aware but lacking action grounding, where the agent correctly identifies the objective but cannot map it to valid action primitives; (D) genuine attempts with execution failure; and (E) initial progress wasted by early errors. Category B dominates: agents demonstrate task comprehension but systematically fail to translate it into correct ScienceWorld actions.",
          " \\label{tab:death-taxonomy}"
```

```json
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\nWe analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms that death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types, with a mean $\\Delta\\text{DR} = +38.2$\\,pp exceeding the included tasks' $+28.7$\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task}).\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:death-decomp}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-task scatter: death rate differential (internal $-$ deterministic) vs.\\ performance gap ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$). $r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$ task types.}\n\\label{fig:decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAcross 245 episodes, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$; excluding death episodes, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, yielding DAF $= 90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$; at the default $P{=}100$). The penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction is 78.9\\% (75 of 95 non-tie labels). Given observed $\\delta = 0.265$ and $P = 100$, the predicted DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}) = 26.5 / 29.37 \\approx 90.3\\%$, closely matching the observed value; DAF is thus a derived quantity once $\\delta$, $P$, and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are known, while the 78.9\\% label fraction is the penalty-independent empirical finding. Of these 75, 54 (72.0\\%) reduce to ties when the death penalty is removed entirely: in those episodes, both modalities score zero without death, so the only reason one strategy outscored the other was avoiding the $-100$ penalty. Death avoidance is not merely the dominant but often the \\emph{sole} discriminating signal between modalities. The remaining 21 death-asymmetry labels involve episodes where the surviving strategy also earned positive score, but the death penalty still accounts for the majority of the score gap.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}\nThe internal strategy dies in 42.9\\% of episodes versus 16.3\\% for deterministic (2.6$\\times$), consistent with the internal strategy's reliance on self-generated observations that compound prediction errors across steps. Per-task, death rate differential correlates with $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\\ref{fig:decomposition}). Since $P$ is constant across all task types, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = P \\cdot \\delta + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ makes $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ a linear function of $\\delta$ with residual $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$; when DAF $\\rightarrow 1$, this residual vanishes and $r \\rightarrow 1$ by construction. The near-perfect correlation is therefore a mathematical consequence of death dominance, not an independent empirical finding. We retain it as an internal consistency check. The correlation drops below significance at $P \\leq 25$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \\texttt{det\\_preferred}, 9 \\texttt{internal\\_preferred}, and 150 \\texttt{no\\_preference}.\n\n\\paragraph{Signal concentration.}\nThe Signal Concentration Index (SCI $= 0.2147$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) confirms low concentration: the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\\%$ of total death-rate differential. One outlier, \\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, has zero deaths ($\\Delta = +9.58$ from non-death scores alone); all 10 remaining types exhibit death-asymmetry-driven routing labels.\n\n\\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis.}\nEpisode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\\% CI $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$ for DAF; per-task CIs confirm $\\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 types. Cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$, resampling task types) yields a wider $[73.0\\%, 100\\%]$; with $K < 40$ this should be interpreted cautiously~\\citep{cameron-2008}. A wild cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$ task clusters, $B{=}9{,}999$) confirms significance at the cluster level ($p < 0.001$, Cohen's $d = 3.02$, 95\\% CI $[0.73, 1.00]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}). Both intervals exclude 50\\%, so death avoidance accounts for a majority of routing signal under resampling. A leave-one-task-out analysis confirms DAF stability: removing any single death-prone task changes DAF by at most 3.0\\,pp (range: 75.9\\%--78.9\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:loto-daf}). Across all 28 death-prone tasks (included and excluded), internal DR exceeds deterministic DR in every case (100\\% directional consistency), with pooled $\\Delta = +37.3\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[29.7\\%, 44.4\\%]$); 15 of 28 are individually significant, with the 13 non-significant cases entirely explained by small sample size ($n{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task-ci}). An 8-seed cross-validation of the router ($47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy, all seeds below chance) confirms that the failure to learn routing generalizes across data splits (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{Seed robustness.}\nA full replication with an independent environment seed (seed$=$123, $N{=}220$) yields DAF $= 89.7\\%$, within 0.7~pp of the primary analysis, with virtually identical $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ ($2.83$ vs.\\ $2.84$) and per-task correlation $r = 0.94$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}).\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nA Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct~\\citep{llama3-2024} replication on 7 task types ($N{=}70$) shows the death-avoidance pattern reproduces and intensifies: internal death rate $34.3\\%$ vs.\\ deterministic $0\\%$ (cf.\\ Qwen: $42.9\\%$ vs.\\ $16.3\\%$), with zero internal-preferred episodes (24 det-preferred, 46 no-preference). With $N{=}70$ and zero internal-preferred labels, this preliminary pilot demonstrates directional consistency of the death mechanism across model families, not full framework replication, which would require a larger sample and independent environment. Both models are in the 7--8B range; $10{\\times}$ scaling results (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) and Reflexion experiments confirm that death dominance persists across capability levels (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}).\n\n\\subsection{Agent Improvement Baselines}\n\\label{sec:agent-improvement}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Death\\% & $N$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\\\\nReflexion ($3{\\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\\\\nDeterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\textit{10 death-prone tasks only:}} \\\\\nDeath-prone baseline & 48.0 & 200 \\\\\nRetry ($5{\\times}$) & 43.5 & 200 \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$ scale) & 34.5 & 200 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under agent improvement methods. Top: all 11 tasks. Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded, 0\\% death at all scales). All interventions reduce death rate but none approaches deterministic-level safety.}\n\\label{tab:agent-improvement}\n\\end{table}\n\nWe test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$ attempts without self-correction) yields marginal reduction on death-prone tasks ($43.5\\%$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$ baseline, $\\Delta = -4.5$~pp). Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} (verbal self-reflection between trials) reduces death to 31.4\\% across all 11 tasks by rescuing 29.3\\% of first-trial deaths, but the gap to deterministic remains 15.1~pp; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\\sim}$35\\%. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$ parameters) reduces death-prone rate to $34.5\\%$ with task-level variation from $+5$~pp (chemistry-mix) to $-31$~pp (lifespan-longest-lived; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}). Recomputing DAF with 72B yields $90.9\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[83.9\\%, 97.3\\%]$), nearly identical to 7B ($90.4\\%$); scaling lowers death rate but leaves death dominance structurally intact.\n\n\\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}\nWe extend both interventions to $N{=}5$ trials on death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat ($48.0\\% \\to 43.5\\%$, $\\Delta = -4.5$~pp over five trials). Reflexion drops steeply to $34.0\\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p < 0.005$) then plateaus ($34$--$36.5\\%$ for $N \\geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$). Retry's per-trial conditional death rate stays constant at ${\\sim}48\\%$; Reflexion's declines to ${\\sim}31\\%$ by Trial~3 (per-task breakdown in Appendix~\\ref{app:dose-response}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Death rate as a function of trial count ($N$) for Retry and Reflexion on 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$). Retry (blue) is nearly flat; Reflexion (red) drops steeply through $N{=}3$ then plateaus. Horizontal lines: 72B scaling baseline ($34.5\\%$) and deterministic baseline ($16.3\\%$). Error bands: Wilson 95\\% CIs.}\n\\label{fig:dose-response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Domain Negative Contrast: MATH}\n\\label{sec:math-contrast}\n\nOn MATH Level~4--5~\\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$ problems, code interpreter as deterministic modality), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy: $73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$ ($+0.5$~pp, McNemar $p = 0.81$, 95\\% CI $[-2.6, +3.6]$~pp). The label distribution is balanced (107 \\texttt{int\\_preferred} vs.\\ 95 \\texttt{det\\_preferred}, 74.6\\% \\texttt{no\\_preference}), ruling out routing signal ${\\geq}3.6$~pp at $\\alpha{=}0.05$. Without catastrophic failure, modality routing confers no advantage.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Environment Contrasts}\n\\label{sec:cross-env}\n\nWe evaluate two additional environments lacking catastrophic death penalties. The ``deterministic'' modality differs by environment: an oracle simulator (ScienceWorld), a code interpreter (MATH), or admissible-action access (ALFWorld). These are not equivalent modality switches: each instantiates ``deterministic computation'' through a qualitatively different mechanism. Results across environments should therefore be interpreted as boundary probes of framework applicability rather than direct replications. The framework's diagnostic value lies in identifying different mechanisms (death avoidance in ScienceWorld, null signal in MATH, information asymmetry in ALFWorld) across heterogeneous settings (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal-reversal}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/signal_reversal.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-environment label distributions. ScienceWorld and ALFWorld both show deterministic preference, but through different mechanisms: death avoidance (ScienceWorld) vs.\\ information asymmetry from admissible-action access (ALFWorld). MATH shows balanced labels, confirming no routing signal without catastrophic failure. ``Deterministic'' denotes qualitatively different modalities per environment (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-env}).}\n\\label{fig:signal-reversal}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{ALFWorld.} ALFWorld~\\citep{alfworld-2021} is a text-based household environment (6 task types, 135 games, max 50 steps). The deterministic strategy additionally receives admissible actions, an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch. The success rate gap is 27.4\\% vs.\\ 3.0\\%, with 35 deterministic-preferred, 2 internal-preferred, and 98 no-preference labels (96 both-fail). The deterministic advantage stems from the information gap rather than death avoidance, a qualitatively different mechanism that the framework diagnoses without modification.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nEnvironment & $N$ & Det\\% & Int\\% & NoPref\\% & Death? \\\\\n\\midrule\nScienceWorld & 245 & 35.1 & 3.7 & 61.2 & Yes ($-100$) \\\\\nMATH & 795 & 12.0 & 13.5 & 74.6 & No \\\\\nALFWorld & 135 & 25.9 & 1.5 & 72.6 & No\\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Cross-environment label distribution. ``Deterministic'' denotes qualitatively different modalities: an oracle simulator providing ground-truth state (ScienceWorld), a code interpreter executing symbolic computation (MATH), or admissible-action access providing action-space pruning (ALFWorld). Only ScienceWorld satisfies all framework assumptions (paired modalities, binary catastrophic outcomes, sparse rewards); MATH and ALFWorld lack catastrophic outcomes and serve as boundary probes. $\\dagger$\\,ALFWorld's deterministic advantage stems from information asymmetry, not death avoidance.}\n\\label{tab:cross-env}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Baselines}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & Score & 95\\% CI & Death \\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\\\\nAlways-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\\\\nRandom & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\\\\nOracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained Router$^\\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 tasks, 245 episodes). $\\dagger$\\,Single random seed (42) per episode pair; see Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness} for 8-seed robustness analysis.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\nAlways-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\\% of decisions matching always-deterministic, an \\emph{oracle degeneracy}: the optimal routing policy closely resembles unconditional deterministic computation.\n\n\\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic Probe}\n\\label{sec:router-diagnostic}\n\nWe probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside; ${\\sim}27.7\\%$ of step-level labels are misaligned with episode-level assignments, further limiting learnability. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with 4-seed configurations shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$, within seed variance). At episode level, only 4B exceeds always-deterministic ($59.0 \\pm 1.9\\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at or below chance. The multi-step nature of routing (10--50 actions with compounding state uncertainty) creates inherent label noise between step-level decisions and episode-level outcomes.\n\n\\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} The 4B router deployed end-to-end ($N{=}245$) scores $-12.03$ (95\\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Paraphrase-based augmentation ($25\\%$--$800\\%$) provides no improvement; the $3.7\\%$ internal-preferred base rate makes real-data collection expensive (Appendices~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}--\\ref{app:ablations}).\n",
        "numLines": 137,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 137
      }
    },
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "2e5f0553-1951-4fec-b435-3da4cd9113a2",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nA Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation with 30 task types, faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.\nToolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.\nMore recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \\cite{mtrouter-2026}.\nConcurrent analyses of when and whether to invoke tools~\\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026} further presuppose diverse routing signals.\nThe shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources (accuracy, latency, capability matching) and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.\nBut no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing decisions in interactive environments. Without such a decomposition, the field cannot distinguish settings where diverse routing signals justify learned routers from those where a single failure mode dominates and a fixed strategy suffices.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{formal diagnostic framework} for answering this question. The framework comprises (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), a penalty-dependent metric that decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding that holds regardless of how death is penalized. Under ScienceWorld's specific penalty structure ($P{=}100$), the penalty-weighted DAF reaches $90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$), consistent with the predicted value from observed $\\delta{=}0.265$ and $P{=}100$.\nThe internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.\nOnce death episodes are removed, the performance gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$. A per-task consistency check ($r = 0.97$) confirms that the near-perfect correlation is a mathematical consequence of death dominance when $P$ is constant (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n\nThis framework addresses a methodological gap.\nThe standard approach to generating routing labels, per-step oracle comparison, degenerates under sparse rewards.\nIn ScienceWorld, non-zero reward arrives only at episode termination; 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical scores regardless of modality, so no step-level signal exists (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\nKeyword-based heuristics also fail structurally, as ScienceWorld actions lack science-specific terminology (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}).\nOur counterfactual approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compares episode outcomes to generate routing labels (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}). The framework is applicable to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes, requiring only paired episode outcomes under two modalities.\n\nTwo cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.\nFirst, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation ($N{=}795$ problems, code interpreter as deterministic modality) confirms no routing signal without catastrophic failure: internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$).\nSecond, a preliminary cross-model pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (7 task types, $N{=}70$) shows direction-consistent death patterns, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.5\\%$ for Qwen; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}).\nAgent improvement baselines confirm that death dominance is robust: retry yields only marginal reduction, while $10{\\times}$ model scaling (72B) and Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} each reduce death rates to ${\\sim}$35\\% through independent mechanisms, yet neither closes the ${\\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{An episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} for decomposing routing signal in interactive environments, with dual penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}) and router training as a diagnostic probe of label informativeness. The framework requires only paired episode outcomes under two modalities with binary catastrophic indicators (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf}, \\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Evidence that death dominance is robust and routing value is near-degenerate}: 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry, with DAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$. The oracle router degenerates to unconditional deterministic computation (96.3\\% decision overlap). Retry, $10{\\times}$ model scaling, and Reflexion each fail to close the ${\\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution. A failure mode taxonomy identifies the root mechanism: 100\\% of internal-strategy deaths stem from a single action-space grounding failure (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}, Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-environment boundary probes}: MATH ($N{=}795$) confirms the predicted null ($p = 0.81$); ALFWorld ($N{=}135$) identifies information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism; a Llama pilot ($N{=}70$) shows cross-model directional consistency. These are boundary probes of framework applicability, not direct replications (\\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\n",
        "numLines": 39,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 39
      }
    },
```

```json
  "attachment": {

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}\nA growing body of work augments language models with external tools \\citep{tool-learning-survey-2024}.\nToolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} teaches LLMs to insert API calls via self-supervised loss filtering; ToolkenGPT \\cite{toolkengpt-2023} represents tools as special tokens; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} composes heterogeneous modules per subtask; ReTool \\cite{retool-2025} applies RL to discover when code execution is preferable to chain-of-thought; ToolACE \\cite{toolace-2025} optimizes function-calling accuracy through synthetic data curation.\nThese focus on \\emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \\emph{what signal drives the routing decision?}\nRecent concurrent work sharpens this question: \\citet{tool-overuse-illusion-2026} identify a cognitive-illusion effect where LLMs default to external tools even when internal knowledge suffices, finding that tool overuse correlates with model uncertainty rather than task difficulty. \\citet{when2tool-2026} show tool-call decisions are encoded in hidden states before reasoning, \\citet{tool-use-tax-2026} quantify a ``tool-use tax,'' and \\citet{to-call-or-not-2026} propose a necessity--utility--affordability framework. Our decomposition provides environment-grounded evidence: in ScienceWorld, 78.9\\% of routing labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.\n\n\\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}\nCogRouter \\cite{cogrouter-2026} adapts reasoning depth per step, ARES \\cite{ares-2026} selects among reasoning effort levels, Mixture-of-Recursions \\cite{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths, and MTRouter \\cite{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn routing.\nRouteLLM \\citep{routellm-2024} learns routers from preference data to select between strong and weak models.\nThese optimize \\emph{how} to route; we reveal \\emph{why} routing helps: in interactive environments, the dominant signal is catastrophic-failure avoidance.\n\n\\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation in RL.}\nPrior work generates counterfactual explanations for RL agents by perturbing individual actions within a fixed policy \\citep{olson2021counterfactual, gajcin2023counterfactual, amitai2024explaining}.\nOur approach differs in both purpose and granularity: we execute two complete episodes under different routing strategies, producing prospective paired comparisons that isolate routing signal \\emph{before} any router is trained.\n\n\\paragraph{LLM Agent Environments and Reasoning.}\nScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; Inner Monologue \\cite{huang2022inner} grounds LLM planning in embodied feedback; Voyager \\cite{wang2023voyager} demonstrates open-ended LLM agents; ALFWorld \\cite{alfworld-2021} provides a complementary household environment; AgentBench \\cite{agentbench-2024} offers systematic evaluation. \\citet{zhou2024webarena} document common failure modes (premature termination, hallucinated actions) in web-based agents, consistent with our finding that state hallucination is the primary internal-strategy failure mechanism. ReAct \\cite{react-2023} serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \\cite{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection; LATS \\cite{lats-2024} adds tree search. SelfCheckGPT \\cite{selfcheckgpt-2023} detects unreliable LLM outputs; our work shows that in interactive environments, such unreliability manifests as catastrophic death.\n\n\\paragraph{Safe RL.}\nSafe RL methods \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,tamar2015cvar,achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021} enforce constraints in MDPs; SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails. Our diagnostic shows modality routing already functions as an implicit safety mechanism in environments with catastrophic failure.\n",
        "numLines": 23,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 23
      }
    },
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "8d47f864-0999-4a6e-a237-d46ce8cfaff7",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically (Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modalities $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\cite{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), where the agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which modality to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$. A reasoning-depth ablation ($n{=}120$) found no significant difference ($p > 0.26$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling assigns routing labels by comparing single-step outcomes under each computation modality. Under sparse terminal rewards, this approach degenerates: on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores regardless of modality, yielding no routing signal. We instead perform episode-level counterfactual analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and modality $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying modality $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the modality choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM) on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two modalities can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score to reflect the asymmetric cost of catastrophic failure in interactive environments. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. The full procedure is given in Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}\n\\label{alg:labeling}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, modalities $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$\n\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}\n    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$\n    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = 0$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{both dead}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt det} > s_{\\tt int}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt int} > s_{\\tt det}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $\\ell$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\nThe algorithm prioritizes survival over score: death asymmetry (lines 5--8) determines preference before score comparison (lines 11--14). Label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. The full gap decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation holds exactly when the surviving strategy scores zero in all death-asymmetry episodes; in our data, the residual is small ($29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$, or $0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction as complementary metrics.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nA complementary question is whether death-avoidance signal is driven by a few outlier task types or distributed systematically. We quantify this via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI) based on the Gini coefficient over per-task routing signal contributions (formal definition in Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). SCI $\\in [0, 1]$: values near 0 indicate uniform distribution (systematic phenomenon); values near 1 indicate concentration in a single type (isolated phenomenon). Results are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n",

        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 99
      }
    },
```

```text
18	\title{Death Avoidance as the Dominant Routing Signal:\\A Counterfactual Analysis on ScienceWorld}
19	
20	\author{Anonymous}
21	
22	\begin{document}
23	\maketitle
24	
25	\input{abstract}
26	\input{introduction}
27	\input{method}

32	
33	\section*{Limitations}
34	
35	\paragraph{Environmental scope.}
36	Our primary analysis uses a single environment (ScienceWorld, 11 of 30 task types) with one backbone (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct); generalization requires further validation. The $-100$ death penalty amplifies DAF; environments with softer penalties would exhibit lower DAF values (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). The 19 excluded task types (63\%) lack routing signal because both strategies typically time out within 30 steps; increasing the step budget could surface non-death-dominated signal that our current setup does not capture. Cross-environment analyses on MATH and ALFWorld (\S\ref{sec:cross-env}, \S\ref{sec:discussion}) provide preliminary supporting evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. The Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct pilot (7 task types, $N{=}70$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}) confirms direction-consistent death patterns, though both models are in the 7--8B parameter range; Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$ scale) reduces death rate but does not eliminate death dominance (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}); validation beyond 72B remains future work. Our quantitative results (DAF $= 90.4\%$, label fractions) are specific to ScienceWorld; the framework's generality claim is methodological (the diagnostic procedure applies to any environment with asymmetric fatal outcomes), not empirical (the specific percentages may differ across environments).
37	
38	\paragraph{Methodological constraints.}
39	Our internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\citep{react-2023}. Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death rate from 42.9\% to 31.4\% (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), but more aggressive self-correction or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\citep{tot-2023} may further reduce internal death rates. Episode-level labels assign a single routing preference to entire episodes, which may be suboptimal for individual steps (particularly those preceding death events). The mislabeling fraction (episodes where the episode-level label disagrees with what step-level oracle labels would assign) is approximately 27.7\%, concentrated in death-asymmetry episodes where early steps may have favored the eventually-dying strategy. Training on pure-strategy trajectories also introduces distribution shift at deployment~\citep{dagger-2011}.
40	
41	\paragraph{Statistical limitations.}
42	Episodes within the same task type are not fully independent because they share environment dynamics, though they use different random seeds. Cluster bootstrap with $K{=}11$ task-type clusters yields wider CIs ($[73.0\%, 100\%]$); with $K < 40$ clusters, these should be interpreted cautiously~\citep{cameron-2008,webb-2023}. A wild cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$, $p < 0.001$; Appendix~\ref{app:wild-cluster}) and 100\% directional consistency across all 28 death-prone tasks (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task-ci}) mitigate this concern.
43	
44	\section*{Ethics Statement}
45	
46	This work uses ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a publicly available text-based simulation environment, and does not involve human subjects or private data. All evaluation environments (ScienceWorld, ALFWorld, MATH) are released under permissive open-source licenses (Apache 2.0 / MIT). All models used (Qwen2.5 family, Llama-3.1-8B) are publicly available for research use. All language models are accessed via standard APIs or publicly released weights. Experiments were conducted on 2$\times$ NVIDIA RTX 5090 (32GB) and 4$\times$ NVIDIA RTX 6000 PRO (96GB), totaling approximately 200 GPU-hours for data collection, router training, and evaluation. Code and data will be released upon acceptance.
47	
48	\bibliography{references}
49	
50	\appendix
51	
52	\section{Signal Concentration Index}
53	\label{app:sci}
54	
55	Let $K$ denote the number of task types and $c_k$ the contribution of task type $k$ to the aggregate routing signal, defined as the fraction of total death-rate differential attributable to task $k$:
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_018dv3mSzEfi2J85N95bvfSS`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We presented a diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents via episode-level counterfactual analysis. Applied to ScienceWorld (245 episodes, 11 task types), 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry; agent improvement baselines (retry, $10{\times}$ scaling, Reflexion) confirm this is robust. Cross-domain contrasts support framework predictions: MATH shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure; ALFWorld identifies information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism. Replication in permadeath environments (NetHack~\citep{nethack-2020}, Crafter~\citep{crafter-2022}), building on recent LLM agent benchmarks \citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024}, and evaluation with stronger backbones are the most direct open directions.
4	
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01CK4bpEb6Nf4sXL2iFQGQFo`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}
5	The router diagnostic (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable features. Cross-task-type transfer to physical-science tasks yields chance-level accuracy (49.2\%, 95\% CI $[42.4\%, 55.9\%]$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}), and 7 of 11 task types contain only deterministic-preferred labels, making majority-class collapse a structural consequence of the label distribution.
6	
7	\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}
8	Four intervention axes all fail to close the gap to deterministic safety (16.3\%): Retry ($43.5\%$ at $N{=}5$), Reflexion ($34.0\%$ at $N{=}3$, plateauing at $34$--$36.5\%$), $10{\times}$ scaling ($34.5\%$, 72B), and action-space filtering ($29.0\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}). All four leave a ${\sim}$13--27~pp residual gap. Reflexion and scaling converge to overlapping 95\% Wilson CIs ($[27.8\%, 40.8\%]$ and $[28.0\%, 41.0\%]$), suggesting a shared structural bound from the lack of ground-truth state access. Prior tool-routing work \citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings; in multi-step environments, the death-rate differential ($\delta = 26.5$~pp) resists all four tested intervention axes.
9	
10	\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}
11	All 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (taxonomy in Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is action-space grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot ground understanding in valid primitives. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\sim}5\%$ \texttt{focus on} rate in both). \texttt{find-non-living-thing}, the sole zero-death task, uses \texttt{focus on} more frequently than any death-prone task: non-living objects are the majority class, so indiscriminate submissions rarely trigger death, confirming that lethality is a joint function of action misuse and answer distribution. Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\% \to 37.0\%$ via prompt warning; $\to 29.0\%$ via action-space filter), with ironic effects where warnings increased \texttt{focus on} salience on identification tasks (Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).
12	
13	\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy.}
14	The oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\% of decisions (score improvement: 2.89 points), confirming near-degenerate optimal routing under death dominance.
15	
16	\paragraph{Scaling and practical implications.}
17	Qwen2.5-72B reduces death-prone death rate to $34.5\%$ yet DAF remains $90.9\%$; the gap to deterministic persists at ${\sim}$18~pp. For practitioners: (1)~identify catastrophic failure mechanisms, (2)~estimate death-rate asymmetry via a ${\sim}$50-episode pilot, (3)~if death-asymmetry labels dominate, a fixed deterministic strategy likely matches a learned router. The MATH negative control ($p = 0.81$) validates this diagnostic.
18	
19	\paragraph{Framework applicability.}
20	The penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). The framework requires paired modality episodes with binary catastrophic outcomes. NetHack~\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, are the most direct replication targets; recent benchmarks \citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide infrastructure.
21	
```
